Specialist AI: JAH Presolved Physics Derivations Librarian

JAH Presolved Physics Derivations Librarian answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedPhysicsDerivationsTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Derive v = u + at from the definition of acceleration JAH-PSPD-000001 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS), and the relation is confirmed by the cited authoritative sources.

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v = u + at from the definition of acceleration'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive v = u + at from the definition of acceleration', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v = u + at from the definition of acceleration'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the relation v = u + at from the definition of acceleration, and check it numerically for u = 5 m/s, a = 2 m/s², t = 3 s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Acceleration is defined as the rate of change of velocity: a = (v − u)/t, where u is the initial velocity and v is the velocity after time t. Step 2: Multiply both sides by t: a×t = v − u. Step 3: Add u to both sides: v = u + at. This is the first equation of motion, valid for constant acceleration. Step 4: Numerical check: v = 5 + 2 × 3 = 5 + 6 = 11 m/s. Step 5: Cross-check: (v − u)/t = (11 − 5)/3 = 2 m/s², which recovers the stated acceleration. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 11 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v = u + at. Numerical check: with u = 5 m/s, a = 2 m/s² and t = 3 s, v = 11 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. v = u + a * t — v is the final velocity (m/s); u is the initial velocity (5 m/s); a is the constant acceleration (2 m/s²); t is the elapsed time (3 s). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains v = 11 m/s, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation is a rearrangement of the definition of acceleration; it is exact whenever the acceleration is constant over the interval. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation is a rearrangement of the definition of acceleration; it is exact whenever the acceleration is constant over the interval. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Demo

The demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints the computed result, a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload run twice, and a Deterministic: MATCH line. Its exact output is stored in expected_output; run it with: python3 build_lane_b.py --demo JAH-PSPD-000001

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
u = 5.0
a = 2.0
t = 3.0
v = u + a * t
_expected_v = 11.0
_check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
_chk_a_check = (v - u) / t
_exp_a_check = 2.0
_ok_a_check = abs(_chk_a_check - _exp_a_check) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"t = {t:.2f} s",
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check a_check: {'PASS' if _ok_a_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_check:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_check:.2f})",
]
payload = f"v={v:.6g}; a_check={_chk_a_check:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive v = u + at from the definition of acceleration — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        u = 5.0
        a = 2.0
        t = 3.0
        v = u + a * t
        _expected_v = 11.0
        _check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
        _chk_a_check = (v - u) / t
        _exp_a_check = 2.0
        _ok_a_check = abs(_chk_a_check - _exp_a_check) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"t = {t:.2f} s",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check a_check: {'PASS' if _ok_a_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_check:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_check:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"v={v:.6g}; a_check={_chk_a_check:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive s = ut + ½at²'. The Problem

Derive the relation s = ut + ½at² for motion under constant acceleration, and check it numerically for u = 5 m/s, a = 2 m/s², t = 3 s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Under constant acceleration the velocity grows linearly from u to v = u + at. Step 2: The average velocity over the interval is (u + v)/2 = (u + u + at)/2 = u + at/2. Step 3: Displacement is average velocity times time: s = (u + at/2) × t. Step 4: Expanding gives s = ut + ½at², the second equation of motion. Step 5: Numerical check: s = 5 × 3 + 0.5 × 2 × 9 = 15 + 9 = 24 m; by average velocity, (5 + 11)/2 × 3 = 24 m, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is s = 24 m.

The Answer

Derived: s = ut + ½at². Numerical check: with u = 5 m/s, a = 2 m/s² and t = 3 s, s = 24 m. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. s = u * t + 0.5 * a * t^2 — s is the displacement (m); u is the initial velocity (5 m/s); a is the constant acceleration (2 m/s²); t is the elapsed time (3 s). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains s = 24 m, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes constant acceleration and motion in a straight line; the average-velocity step is exact only because the velocity grows linearly. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
u = 5.0
a = 2.0
t = 3.0
v = u + a * t
_expected_v = 11.0
_check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
s = u * t + 0.5 * a * t ** 2
_expected_s = 24.0
_check_s = abs(s - _expected_s) <= 0.01
_chk_s_avg = (u + v) / 2.0 * t
_exp_s_avg = 24.0

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive s = ut + ½at² — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        u = 5.0
        a = 2.0
        t = 3.0
        v = u + a * t
        _expected_v = 11.0
        _check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
        s = u * t + 0.5 * a * t ** 2
        _expected_s = 24.0
        _check_s = abs(s - _expected_s) <= 0.01
        _chk_s_avg = (u + v) / 2.0 * t
        _exp_s_avg = 24.0
        _ok_s_avg = abs(_chk_s_avg - _exp_s_avg) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"t = {t:.2f} s",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
            f"s = {s:.2f} m",
            f"Check s against stated value {_expected_s:.2f} m: {'PASS' if _check_s else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check s_avg: {'PASS' if _ok_s_avg else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_s_avg:.2f}, expected {_exp_s_avg:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"v={v:.6g}; s={s:.6g}; s_avg={_chk_s_avg:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Derive v² = u² + 2as JAH-PSPD-000003 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS), and the relation is confirmed by the cited authoritative sources.

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v² = u² + 2as'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive v² = u² + 2as', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive v² = u² + 2as'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the relation v² = u² + 2as for motion under constant acceleration, and check it numerically for u = 5 m/s, a = 2 m/s², s = 24 m.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Start from v = u + at and s = ut + ½at². Step 2: Solve the first for t: t = (v − u)/a. Step 3: Substitute into the second: s = u(v − u)/a + a(v − u)²/(2a²). Step 4: Multiply through by 2a and simplify: 2as = 2u(v − u) + (v − u)² = v² − u². Step 5: Rearranging gives v² = u² + 2as. Numerical check: v² = 25 + 2 × 2 × 24 = 121, so v = 11 m/s. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 11 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v² = u² + 2as. Numerical check: with u = 5 m/s, a = 2 m/s² and s = 24 m, v² = 121, so v = 11 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. v^2 = u^2 + 2 * a * s — v is the final velocity (m/s); u is the initial velocity (5 m/s); a is the constant acceleration (2 m/s²); s is the displacement (24 m). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains v = 11 m/s, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes constant acceleration in a straight line; it is the time-free equation of motion and fails if the acceleration varies. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes constant acceleration in a straight line; it is the time-free equation of motion and fails if the acceleration varies. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Derive F = ma from momentum JAH-PSPD-000004 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS), and the relation is confirmed by the cited authoritative sources.

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive F = ma from momentum'. History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive F = ma from momentum'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive F = ma from the definition of momentum and Newton's second law, and check it numerically for m = 2 kg, a velocity change of 6 m/s in 3 s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Momentum is p = mv. Newton's second law states that force is the rate of change of momentum: F = Δp/Δt. Step 2: For a constant mass, Δp = m × Δv, so F = m × Δv/Δt. Step 3: But Δv/Δt is the acceleration a, so F = ma. Step 4: Numerical check: a = 6/3 = 2 m/s², so F = 2 × 2 = 4 N. Step 5: Cross-check: Δp = 2 × 6 = 12 kg·m/s, and F = 12/3 = 4 N, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is F = 4 N.

The Answer

Derived: F = ma. Numerical check: with m = 2 kg and a = 2 m/s², F = 4 N. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. F = m * a — F is the force (N); m is the mass (2 kg); a is the acceleration (2 m/s²), equal to Δv/Δt with Δv = 6 m/s and Δt = 3 s. Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains F = 4 N, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
m = 2.0
delta_v = 6.0
delta_t = 3.0
a = delta_v / delta_t
_expected_a = 2.0
_check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
F = m * a
_expected_F = 4.0
_check_F = abs(F - _expected_F) <= 0.01
_chk_F_momentum = m * delta_v / delta_t
_exp_F_momentum = 4.0
_ok_F_momentum = abs(_chk_F_momentum - _exp_F_momentum) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
    f"delta_v = {delta_v:.2f} m/s",
    f"delta_t = {delta_t:.2f} s",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
    f"F = {F:.2f} N",
    f"Check F against stated value {_expected_F:.2f} N: {'PASS' if _check_F else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check F_momentum: {'PASS' if _ok_F_momentum else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_F_momentum:.2f}, expected {_exp_F_momentum:.2f})",
]
payload = f"a={a:.6g}; F={F:.6g}; F_momentum={_chk_F_momentum:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)

_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive F = ma from momentum — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m = 2.0
        delta_v = 6.0
        delta_t = 3.0
        a = delta_v / delta_t
        _expected_a = 2.0
        _check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
        F = m * a
        _expected_F = 4.0
        _check_F = abs(F - _expected_F) <= 0.01
        _chk_F_momentum = m * delta_v / delta_t
        _exp_F_momentum = 4.0
        _ok_F_momentum = abs(_chk_F_momentum - _exp_F_momentum) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
            f"delta_v = {delta_v:.2f} m/s",
            f"delta_t = {delta_t:.2f} s",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
            f"F = {F:.2f} N",
            f"Check F against stated value {_expected_F:.2f} N: {'PASS' if _check_F else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check F_momentum: {'PASS' if _ok_F_momentum else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_F_momentum:.2f}, expected {_exp_F_momentum:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"a={a:.6g}; F={F:.6g}; F_momentum={_chk_F_momentum:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Derive kinetic energy ½mv² from work JAH-PSPD-000005 · simple

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive kinetic energy ½mv² from work'. History

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive kinetic energy ½mv² from work'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the kinetic energy formula KE = ½mv² from the definition of work, and check it numerically for m = 2 kg, v = 10 m/s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Work is force times distance: W = F × s. For a constant force from rest, F = ma and v² = 2as, so s = v²/(2a). Step 2: Substituting: W = ma × v²/(2a) = ½mv². Step 3: The work done accelerating the mass becomes its kinetic energy: KE = ½mv². Step 4: Numerical check: KE = 0.5 × 2 × 10² = 100 J. Step 5: Cross-check: a force of 20 N acting over 5 m does 100 J of work, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is KE = 100 J.

The Answer

Derived: KE = ½mv². Numerical check: with m = 2 kg and v = 10 m/s, KE = 100 J. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. KE = 0.5 * m * v^2 — KE is the kinetic energy (J); m is the mass (2 kg); v is the speed (10 m/s). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains KE = 100 J, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes a constant force and non-relativistic speeds; at speeds near light the relativistic formula must be used, and the work-energy step assumes the force acts along the motion. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes a constant force and non-relativistic speeds; at speeds near light the relativistic formula must be used, and the work-energy step assumes the force acts along the motion. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

The Problem

Derive the gravitational potential energy formula GPE = mgh from the definition of work, and check it numerically for m = 5 kg, h = 10 m (g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Lifting a mass m through a height h against gravity requires a force equal to its weight, mg. Step 2: Work is force times distance: W = mg × h. Step 3: That work is stored as gravitational potential energy: GPE = mgh, measured relative to the starting height. Step 4: Numerical check: GPE = 5 × 9.81 × 10 = 490.5 J. Step 5: Cross-check: the weight is 49.05 N and the lift is 10 m, giving 49.05 × 10 = 490.5 J, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is GPE = 490.5 J.

The Answer

Derived: GPE = mgh. Numerical check: with m = 5 kg, g = 9.81 m/s² and h = 10 m, GPE = 490.5 J. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive gravitational potential energy mgh', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive the gravitational potential energy formula GPE = mgh from the definition of work, and check it numerically for m = 5 kg, h = 10 m (g = 9.81 m/s²).

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Lifting a mass m through a height h against gravity requires a force equal to its weight, mg. Step 2: Work is force times distance: W = mg × h. Step 3: That work is stored as gravitational potential energy: GPE = mgh, measured relative to the starting height. Step 4: Numerical check: GPE = 5 × 9.81 × 10 = 490.5 J. Step 5: Cross-check: the weight is 49.05 N and the lift is 10 m, giving 49.05 × 10 = 490.5 J, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is GPE = 490.5 J.

Dataset

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
m = 5.0
g = 9.81
h = 10.0
GPE = m * g * h
_expected_GPE = 490.5
_check_GPE = abs(GPE - _expected_GPE) <= 0.01
_chk_W_lift = m * g * h
_exp_W_lift = 490.5
_ok_W_lift = abs(_chk_W_lift - _exp_W_lift) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
    f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"h = {h:.2f} m",
    f"GPE = {GPE:.2f} J",
    f"Check GPE against stated value {_expected_GPE:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_GPE else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check W_lift: {'PASS' if _ok_W_lift else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_W_lift:.2f}, expected {_exp_W_lift:.2f})",
]
payload = f"GPE={GPE:.6g}; W_lift={_chk_W_lift:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive gravitational potential energy mgh — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m = 5.0
        g = 9.81
        h = 10.0
        GPE = m * g * h
        _expected_GPE = 490.5
        _check_GPE = abs(GPE - _expected_GPE) <= 0.01
        _chk_W_lift = m * g * h
        _exp_W_lift = 490.5
        _ok_W_lift = abs(_chk_W_lift - _exp_W_lift) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
            f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"h = {h:.2f} m",
            f"GPE = {GPE:.2f} J",
            f"Check GPE against stated value {_expected_GPE:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_GPE else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check W_lift: {'PASS' if _ok_W_lift else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_W_lift:.2f}, expected {_exp_W_lift:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"GPE={GPE:.6g}; W_lift={_chk_W_lift:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

Derive the period of a simple pendulum T = 2π√(L/g) JAH-PSPD-000007 · compounded

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the period of a simple pendulum T = 2π√(L/g)'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the period T = 2π√(L/g) of a simple pendulum, and check it numerically for L = 1 m (g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: For small angles the restoring torque on a pendulum of length L is approximately −mgL×θ, where θ is the angular displacement. Step 2: The equation of motion is I×θ-double-dot = −mgL×θ with I = mL², giving θ-double-dot = −(g/L)×θ. Step 3: This is simple harmonic motion with angular frequency ω = √(g/L). Step 4: The period is T = 2π/ω = 2π√(L/g). Step 5: Numerical check: T = 2π × √(1/9.81) = 2π × 0.3193 = 2.01 s; inverting, g = 4π²L/T² recovers 9.81 m/s². That completes the worked solution; the answer is T = 2.01 s.

The Answer

Derived: T = 2π√(L/g). Numerical check: with L = 1 m and g = 9.81 m/s², T = 2.01 s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. T = 2 * pi * sqrt(L / g) — T is the period (s); L is the pendulum length (1 m); g is the acceleration due to gravity (9.81 m/s²); pi is the circle constant. Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains T = 2.01 s, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation uses the small-angle approximation (sin θ ≈ θ); for large amplitudes the period grows, and air resistance and the mass of the string are neglected. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation uses the small-angle approximation (sin θ ≈ θ); for large amplitudes the period grows, and air resistance and the mass of the string are neglected. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

import math
L = 1.0
g = 9.81
T = 2.0 * math.pi * math.sqrt(L / g)
_expected_T = 2.01

_chk_g_check = 4.0 * math.pi ** 2 * L / T ** 2
_exp_g_check = 9.81
_ok_g_check = abs(_chk_g_check - _exp_g_check) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"L = {L:.2f} m",
    f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"T = {T:.2f} s",
    f"Check T against stated value {_expected_T:.2f} s: {'PASS' if _check_T else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check g_check: {'PASS' if _ok_g_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_g_check:.2f}, expected {_exp_g_check:.2f})",
]

for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the period of a simple pendulum T = 2π√(L/g) — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        L = 1.0
        g = 9.81
        T = 2.0 * math.pi * math.sqrt(L / g)
        _expected_T = 2.01
        _check_T = abs(T - _expected_T) <= 0.01
        _chk_g_check = 4.0 * math.pi ** 2 * L / T ** 2
        _exp_g_check = 9.81
        _ok_g_check = abs(_chk_g_check - _exp_g_check) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"L = {L:.2f} m",
            f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"T = {T:.2f} s",
            f"Check T against stated value {_expected_T:.2f} s: {'PASS' if _check_T else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check g_check: {'PASS' if _ok_g_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_g_check:.2f}, expected {_exp_g_check:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"T={T:.6g}; g_check={_chk_g_check:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive centripetal acceleration a = v²/r'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the centripetal acceleration a = v²/r for uniform circular motion, and check it numerically for v = 10 m/s, r = 5 m.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: In uniform circular motion the velocity vector turns through the same angle as the radius vector, staying perpendicular to it. Step 2: Over a small angle Δθ the change in velocity is Δv = v × Δθ, by similar triangles between the velocity and position diagrams. Step 3: Dividing by Δt and taking the limit gives a = v × ω, where ω = Δθ/Δt is the angular speed. Step 4: Since ω = v/r, the acceleration is a = v²/r, directed towards the centre. Step 5: Numerical check: a = 10²/5 = 20 m/s²; by angular speed, ω = 10/5 = 2 rad/s and a = ω²r = 4 × 5 = 20 m/s², agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is a = 20 m/s².

The Answer

Derived: a = v²/r. Numerical check: with v = 10 m/s and r = 5 m, a = 20 m/s². That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. a = v^2 / r — a is the centripetal acceleration (m/s²); v is the speed (10 m/s); r is the radius (5 m); omega is the angular speed (2 rad/s). History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive centripetal acceleration a = v²/r', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive the centripetal acceleration a = v²/r for uniform circular motion, and check it numerically for v = 10 m/s, r = 5 m.

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: In uniform circular motion the velocity vector turns through the same angle as the radius vector, staying perpendicular to it. Step 2: Over a small angle Δθ the change in velocity is Δv = v × Δθ, by similar triangles between the velocity and position diagrams. Step 3: Dividing by Δt and taking the limit gives a = v × ω, where ω = Δθ/Δt is the angular speed. Step 4: Since ω = v/r, the acceleration is a = v²/r, directed towards the centre. Step 5: Numerical check: a = 10²/5 = 20 m/s²; by angular speed, ω = 10/5 = 2 rad/s and a = ω²r = 4 × 5 = 20 m/s², agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is a = 20 m/s².

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
v = 10.0
r = 5.0
a = v ** 2 / r
_expected_a = 20.0
_check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
_chk_a_omega = (v / r) ** 2 * r
_exp_a_omega = 20.0
_ok_a_omega = abs(_chk_a_omega - _exp_a_omega) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"r = {r:.2f} m",
    f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check a_omega: {'PASS' if _ok_a_omega else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_omega:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_omega:.2f})",
]
payload = f"a={a:.6g}; a_omega={_chk_a_omega:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive centripetal acceleration a = v²/r — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        v = 10.0
        r = 5.0
        a = v ** 2 / r
        _expected_a = 20.0
        _check_a = abs(a - _expected_a) <= 0.01
        _chk_a_omega = (v / r) ** 2 * r
        _exp_a_omega = 20.0
        _ok_a_omega = abs(_chk_a_omega - _exp_a_omega) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"r = {r:.2f} m",
            f"a = {a:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"Check a against stated value {_expected_a:.2f} m/s^2: {'PASS' if _check_a else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check a_omega: {'PASS' if _ok_a_omega else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_a_omega:.2f}, expected {_exp_a_omega:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"a={a:.6g}; a_omega={_chk_a_omega:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")

Derive escape velocity v = √(2GM/R) JAH-PSPD-000009 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS), and the relation is confirmed by the cited authoritative sources.

History

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive escape velocity v = √(2GM/R)'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the escape velocity v = √(2GM/R) from energy conservation, and check it numerically for the Earth (G = 6.674×10⁻¹¹, M = 5.972×10²⁴ kg, R = 6.371×10⁶ m).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A body escapes when its total mechanical energy at infinity is zero: kinetic plus potential energy must sum to zero at the surface. Step 2: Kinetic energy is ½mv² and gravitational potential energy at the surface is −GMm/R. Step 3: Setting the sum to zero: ½mv² = GMm/R. Step 4: Cancelling m and solving gives v = √(2GM/R). Step 5: Numerical check: v = √(2 × 6.674×10⁻¹¹ × 5.972×10²⁴ / 6.371×10⁶) = 11,186 m/s; the kinetic energy per unit mass, ½v², equals GM/R, confirming the energy balance. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 11,186 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v = √(2GM/R). Numerical check: for the Earth, v = 11,186 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. v = sqrt(2 * G * M / R) — v is the escape velocity (m/s); G is the gravitational constant (6.674×10⁻¹¹); M is the Earth's mass (5.972×10²⁴ kg); R is the Earth's radius (6.371×10⁶ m). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains v = 11,186 m/s, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation neglects air resistance and the rotation of the Earth, and it gives the speed needed at the surface with no further thrust; a real launch also fights drag and gravity losses. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

The Problem

Derive Kepler's third law (T² proportional to a³) from circular orbital motion and Newton's law of gravitation, and check it numerically for the Earth's orbit (T = 3.156×10⁷ s, a = 1.496×10¹¹ m).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: For a circular orbit, gravity provides the centripetal force: GMm/r² = mv²/r. Step 2: Cancelling m and one factor of r gives v² = GM/r. Step 3: The orbital speed is v = 2πr/T, so (2πr/T)² = GM/r. Step 4: Rearranging gives T² = (4π²/GM) × r³: the square of the period is proportional to the cube of the orbital radius. Step 5: Numerical check: T²/a³ = (3.156×10⁷)²/(1.496×10¹¹)³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³, and 4π²/(G×M_sun) gives the same value, confirming the law. That completes the worked solution; the answer is T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³.

The Answer

Derived: T² = (4π²/GM)a³. Numerical check: for the Earth, T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³, matching 4π²/(GM_sun). That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. T^2 = (4 * pi^2 / (G * M)) * a^3 — T is the orbital period (3.156×10⁷ s); a is the orbital radius (1.496×10¹¹ m); G is the gravitational constant; M is the Sun's mass (1.989×10³⁰ kg). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes a circular orbit and a central mass much larger than the orbiter; elliptical orbits use the semi-major axis, and mutual perturbations are neglected. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes a circular orbit and a central mass much larger than the orbiter; elliptical orbits use the semi-major axis, and mutual perturbations are neglected. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. Problem

Method

That completes the worked solution; the answer is T²/a³ = 2.98×10⁻¹⁹ s²/m³.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
T = 31560000.0
a = 149600000000.0
G = 6.674e-11
M_sun = 1.989e+30
ratio = T ** 2 / a ** 3
_expected_ratio = 2.98e-19
_check_ratio = abs(ratio - _expected_ratio) <= 1e-21
_chk_ratio_theory = 4.0 * math.pi ** 2 / (G * M_sun)
_exp_ratio_theory = 2.98e-19
_ok_ratio_theory = abs(_chk_ratio_theory - _exp_ratio_theory) <= 1e-21
lines = [
    f"T = {T:.3e} s",
    f"a = {a:.3e} m",
    f"G = {G:.3e} SI",
    f"M_sun = {M_sun:.3e} kg",
    f"ratio = {ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3",
    f"Check ratio against stated value {_expected_ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3: {'PASS' if _check_ratio else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check ratio_theory: {'PASS' if _ok_ratio_theory else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_ratio_theory:.2e}, expected {_exp_ratio_theory:.2e})",
]
payload = f"ratio={ratio:.6g}; ratio_theory={_chk_ratio_theory:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive Kepler's third law from circular orbit and gravitation — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        T = 31560000.0
        a = 149600000000.0
        G = 6.674e-11
        M_sun = 1.989e+30
        ratio = T ** 2 / a ** 3
        _expected_ratio = 2.98e-19
        _check_ratio = abs(ratio - _expected_ratio) <= 1e-21
        _chk_ratio_theory = 4.0 * math.pi ** 2 / (G * M_sun)
        _exp_ratio_theory = 2.98e-19
        _ok_ratio_theory = abs(_chk_ratio_theory - _exp_ratio_theory) <= 1e-21
        lines = [
            f"T = {T:.3e} s",
            f"a = {a:.3e} m",
            f"G = {G:.3e} SI",
            f"M_sun = {M_sun:.3e} kg",
            f"ratio = {ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3",
            f"Check ratio against stated value {_expected_ratio:.2e} s^2/m^3: {'PASS' if _check_ratio else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check ratio_theory: {'PASS' if _ok_ratio_theory else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_ratio_theory:.2e}, expected {_exp_ratio_theory:.2e})",
        ]
        payload = f"ratio={ratio:.6g}; ratio_theory={_chk_ratio_theory:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive pressure variation with depth P = P₀ + ρgh'. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the pressure variation with depth P = P₀ + ρgh for a static fluid, and check it numerically for water at h = 5 m (P₀ = 101,325 Pa, ρ = 1,000 kg/m³, g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Consider a column of fluid of height h and cross-sectional area A. Step 2: Its weight is ρ × A × h × g, and pressure is force over area, so the extra pressure at depth h is ρgh. Step 3: Adding the surface pressure P₀ gives P = P₀ + ρgh. Step 4: Numerical check: P = 101,325 + 1,000 × 9.81 × 5 = 101,325 + 49,050 = 150,375 Pa. Step 5: Cross-check: the added pressure ρgh alone is 49,050 Pa, agreeing with the hydrostatic calculation. That completes the worked solution; the answer is P = 150,375 Pa.

The Answer

Derived: P = P₀ + ρgh. Numerical check: at 5 m depth in water, P = 150,375 Pa. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

        ]

        return lines, payload

Derive the work–energy theorem JAH-PSPD-000012 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS), and the relation is confirmed by the cited authoritative sources.

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the work–energy theorem'. History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the work–energy theorem', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the work–energy theorem'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the work–energy theorem (net work equals the change in kinetic energy), and check it numerically for m = 2 kg accelerating from 3 m/s to 7 m/s.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Work is W = F × s. With F = ma and v² = u² + 2as, the distance is s = (v² − u²)/(2a). Step 2: Substituting: W = ma × (v² − u²)/(2a) = ½m(v² − u²). Step 3: But ½mv² is the final kinetic energy and ½mu² is the initial kinetic energy, so W = KE_final − KE_initial. Step 4: Numerical check: W = 0.5 × 2 × (49 − 9) = 40 J. Step 5: Cross-check: KE_final = 49 J and KE_initial = 9 J, a difference of 40 J, agreeing. That completes the worked solution; the answer is W = 40 J.

The Answer

Derived: W_net = ΔKE. Numerical check: for m = 2 kg from 3 m/s to 7 m/s, the net work is 40 J. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. W = 0.5 * m * (v^2 - u^2) — W is the net work (J); m is the mass (2 kg); v is the final speed (7 m/s); u is the initial speed (3 m/s). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains W = 40 J, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The theorem holds for the net work of all forces; it is exact in classical mechanics but must be replaced by the relativistic form at high speeds, and it tracks energy, not the path taken. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The theorem holds for the net work of all forces; it is exact in classical mechanics but must be replaced by the relativistic form at high speeds, and it tracks energy, not the path taken. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
m = 2.0
u = 3.0
v = 7.0
W = 0.5 * m * (v ** 2 - u ** 2)
_expected_W = 40.0
_check_W = abs(W - _expected_W) <= 0.01
_chk_KE_diff = 0.5 * m * v ** 2 - 0.5 * m * u ** 2
_exp_KE_diff = 40.0
_ok_KE_diff = abs(_chk_KE_diff - _exp_KE_diff) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
    f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"W = {W:.2f} J",
    f"Check W against stated value {_expected_W:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_W else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check KE_diff: {'PASS' if _ok_KE_diff else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_KE_diff:.2f}, expected {_exp_KE_diff:.2f})",
]
payload = f"W={W:.6g}; KE_diff={_chk_KE_diff:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the work–energy theorem — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m = 2.0
        u = 3.0
        v = 7.0
        W = 0.5 * m * (v ** 2 - u ** 2)
        _expected_W = 40.0
        _check_W = abs(W - _expected_W) <= 0.01
        _chk_KE_diff = 0.5 * m * v ** 2 - 0.5 * m * u ** 2
        _exp_KE_diff = 40.0
        _ok_KE_diff = abs(_chk_KE_diff - _exp_KE_diff) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m = {m:.2f} kg",
            f"u = {u:.2f} m/s",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"W = {W:.2f} J",
            f"Check W against stated value {_expected_W:.2f} J: {'PASS' if _check_W else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check KE_diff: {'PASS' if _ok_KE_diff else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_KE_diff:.2f}, expected {_exp_KE_diff:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"W={W:.6g}; KE_diff={_chk_KE_diff:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive conservation of momentum from Newton's third law'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive conservation of momentum from Newton's third law, and check it numerically for a perfectly inelastic collision: m1 = 2 kg at 3 m/s strikes m2 = 3 kg at rest, and they stick together.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Newton's third law says the force on body 1 from body 2 is equal and opposite to the force on body 2 from body 1. Step 2: Over the collision time Δt, the impulses are therefore equal and opposite: Δp1 = −Δp2. Step 3: So the total momentum change is zero: p1 + p2 is the same before and after. Step 4: Numerical check: before, p = 2 × 3 + 3 × 0 = 6 kg·m/s. After, the combined mass is 5 kg, so v' = 6/5 = 1.2 m/s and p = 5 × 1.2 = 6 kg·m/s. Step 5: Momentum is conserved; kinetic energy is not (this collision is inelastic). That completes the worked solution; the answer is p = 6 kg·m/s before and after; v' = 1.2 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: total momentum is conserved in a collision. Numerical check: momentum before is 6 kg·m/s and after is 6 kg·m/s, with a common final speed of 1.2 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. Conservation holds when no external impulse acts during the collision; gravity and friction are neglected over the short collision time, and the derivation assumes classical (non-relativistic) mechanics. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
m1 = 2.0
v1 = 3.0
m2 = 3.0
v2 = 0.0
p_before = m1 * v1 + m2 * v2
_expected_p_before = 6.0
_check_p_before = abs(p_before - _expected_p_before) <= 0.01
v_final = p_before / (m1 + m2)
_expected_v_final = 1.2
_check_v_final = abs(v_final - _expected_v_final) <= 0.01
_chk_p_after = (m1 + m2) * v_final
_exp_p_after = 6.0
_ok_p_after = abs(_chk_p_after - _exp_p_after) <= 0.01
lines = [

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive conservation of momentum from Newton's third law — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m1 = 2.0
        v1 = 3.0
        m2 = 3.0
        v2 = 0.0
        p_before = m1 * v1 + m2 * v2
        _expected_p_before = 6.0
        _check_p_before = abs(p_before - _expected_p_before) <= 0.01
        v_final = p_before / (m1 + m2)
        _expected_v_final = 1.2
        _check_v_final = abs(v_final - _expected_v_final) <= 0.01
        _chk_p_after = (m1 + m2) * v_final
        _exp_p_after = 6.0
        _ok_p_after = abs(_chk_p_after - _exp_p_after) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"m1 = {m1:.2f} kg",
            f"v1 = {v1:.2f} m/s",
            f"m2 = {m2:.2f} kg",
            f"v2 = {v2:.2f} m/s",
            f"p_before = {p_before:.2f} kg m/s",
            f"Check p_before against stated value {_expected_p_before:.2f} kg m/s: {'PASS' if _check_p_before else 'FAIL'}",
            f"v_final = {v_final:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v_final against stated value {_expected_v_final:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v_final else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check p_after: {'PASS' if _ok_p_after else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_p_after:.2f}, expected {_exp_p_after:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"p_before={p_before:.6g}; v_final={v_final:.6g}; p_after={_chk_p_after:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Derive Snell's law from Fermat's principle JAH-PSPD-000014 · compounded

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive Snell's law from Fermat's principle'. History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily.
