Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive Snell's law from Fermat's principle'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive Snell's law from Fermat's principle of least time, and check it numerically for light passing from air (n1 = 1) into glass (n2 = 1.5) at an incidence angle of 30°.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Fermat's principle: light takes the path of least travel time between two points. Step 2: For a ray crossing a flat interface, the travel time is a function of the crossing point; minimising it with respect to that point gives the condition n1×sin(θ1) = n2×sin(θ2). Step 3: That condition is Snell's law: the sines of the angles are in inverse ratio to the refractive indices. Step 4: Numerical check: sin(θ2) = (1/1.5) × sin(30°) = 0.6667 × 0.5 = 0.3333. Step 5: So θ2 = arcsin(0.3333) = 19.47°. That completes the worked solution; the answer is θ2 = 19.47°.

The Answer

Derived: n1×sin(θ1) = n2×sin(θ2). Numerical check: from air into glass at 30° incidence, the refraction angle is 19.47°. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. n1 * sin(theta1) = n2 * sin(theta2) — n1 and n2 are the refractive indices (1 and 1.5); theta1 is the incidence angle (30°); theta2 is the refraction angle (19.47°). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains θ2 = 19.47°, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes a sharp flat interface, homogeneous media, and geometric optics (wavelength small compared with the geometry); it does not cover diffraction or total internal reflection beyond the critical angle. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes a sharp flat interface, homogeneous media, and geometric optics (wavelength small compared with the geometry); it does not cover diffraction or total internal reflection beyond the critical angle. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. import hashlib
import math
n1 = 1.0
n2 = 1.5
theta1 = 30.0
sin_theta2 = n1 / n2 * math.sin(math.radians(theta1))
_expected_sin_theta2 = 0.3333
_check_sin_theta2 = abs(sin_theta2 - _expected_sin_theta2) <= 0.001
theta2 = math.degrees(math.asin(sin_theta2))
_expected_theta2 = 19.47
_check_theta2 = abs(theta2 - _expected_theta2) <= 0.01
_chk_snell_lhs = n1 * math.sin(math.radians(theta1))
_exp_snell_lhs = 0.5
_ok_snell_lhs = abs(_chk_snell_lhs - _exp_snell_lhs) <= 0.001
lines = [
    f"n1 = {n1:.2f} ",
    f"n2 = {n2:.2f} ",
    f"theta1 = {theta1:.2f} deg",
    f"sin_theta2 = {sin_theta2:.4f} ",
    f"Check sin_theta2 against stated value {_expected_sin_theta2:.4f} : {'PASS' if _check_sin_theta2 else 'FAIL'}",
    f"theta2 = {theta2:.2f} deg",
    f"Check theta2 against stated value {_expected_theta2:.2f} deg: {'PASS' if _check_theta2 else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check snell_lhs: {'PASS' if _ok_snell_lhs else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_snell_lhs:.4f}, expected {_exp_snell_lhs:.4f})",
]
payload = f"sin_theta2={sin_theta2:.6g}; theta2={theta2:.6g}; snell_lhs={_chk_snell_lhs:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. The Problem

Derive the thin-lens equation 1/f = 1/do + 1/di from ray geometry, and check it numerically for a lens of focal length f = 10 cm with an object at do = 30 cm.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Trace two rays from the top of the object: one through the centre (undeflected) and one parallel to the axis (refracted through the focal point). Step 2: Similar triangles from the object, the lens, and the image give the magnification relations h_i/h_o = d_i/d_o and h_i/h_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 3: Equating the two expressions for the magnification gives d_i/d_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 4: Cross-multiplying and dividing by f×d_o×d_i gives 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i. Step 5: Numerical check: 1/d_i = 1/10 − 1/30 = 0.0667 per cm, so d_i = 15 cm. That completes the worked solution; the answer is di = 15 cm.

The Answer

Derived: 1/f = 1/do + 1/di. Numerical check: with f = 10 cm and do = 30 cm, the image forms at di = 15 cm. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the lens equation 1/f = 1/do + 1/di', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive the thin-lens equation 1/f = 1/do + 1/di from ray geometry, and check it numerically for a lens of focal length f = 10 cm with an object at do = 30 cm.

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Trace two rays from the top of the object: one through the centre (undeflected) and one parallel to the axis (refracted through the focal point). Step 2: Similar triangles from the object, the lens, and the image give the magnification relations h_i/h_o = d_i/d_o and h_i/h_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 3: Equating the two expressions for the magnification gives d_i/d_o = (d_i − f)/f. Step 4: Cross-multiplying and dividing by f×d_o×d_i gives 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i. Step 5: Numerical check: 1/d_i = 1/10 − 1/30 = 0.0667 per cm, so d_i = 15 cm. That completes the worked solution; the answer is di = 15 cm.

Dataset

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
f = 10.0
do = 30.0
di = 1.0 / (1.0 / f - 1.0 / do)
_expected_di = 15.0
_check_di = abs(di - _expected_di) <= 0.01
_chk_lens_lhs = 1.0 / f
_exp_lens_lhs = 0.1
_ok_lens_lhs = abs(_chk_lens_lhs - _exp_lens_lhs) <= 0.001
lines = [
    f"f = {f:.2f} cm",
    f"do = {do:.2f} cm",
    f"di = {di:.2f} cm",
    f"Check di against stated value {_expected_di:.2f} cm: {'PASS' if _check_di else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check lens_lhs: {'PASS' if _ok_lens_lhs else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_lens_lhs:.4f}, expected {_exp_lens_lhs:.4f})",
]
payload = f"di={di:.6g}; lens_lhs={_chk_lens_lhs:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the lens equation 1/f = 1/do + 1/di — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        f = 10.0
        do = 30.0
        di = 1.0 / (1.0 / f - 1.0 / do)
        _expected_di = 15.0
        _check_di = abs(di - _expected_di) <= 0.01

        _exp_lens_lhs = 0.1
        _ok_lens_lhs = abs(_chk_lens_lhs - _exp_lens_lhs) <= 0.001
        lines = [
            f"f = {f:.2f} cm",
            f"do = {do:.2f} cm",
            f"di = {di:.2f} cm",
            f"Check di against stated value {_expected_di:.2f} cm: {'PASS' if _check_di else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check lens_lhs: {'PASS' if _ok_lens_lhs else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_lens_lhs:.4f}, expected {_exp_lens_lhs:.4f})",
        ]
        payload = f"di={di:.6g}; lens_lhs={_chk_lens_lhs:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":

The Problem

Derive the Doppler shift formula for sound and check it numerically for a 440 Hz source approaching a stationary observer at 20 m/s (speed of sound 343 m/s).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A source moving towards the observer at speed v_s emits each crest from a point closer than the last, compressing the wavelength. Step 2: In one period the source advances v_s×T, so the observed wavelength is λ' = λ − v_s×T = (v − v_s)/f. Step 3: The observed frequency is f' = v/λ' = f × v/(v − v_s). Step 4: Numerical check: f' = 440 × 343/(343 − 20) = 440 × 1.0619 = 467.24 Hz. Step 5: The pitch rises by about 27 Hz as the source approaches. That completes the worked solution; the answer is f' = 467.24 Hz.

The Answer

Derived: f' = f×v/(v − v_s) for an approaching source. Numerical check: a 440 Hz source approaching at 20 m/s is heard at 467.24 Hz. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the Doppler shift formula for sound — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

        v_s = 20.0

        ]
        payload = f"f_obs={f_obs:.6g}; lambda_obs={_chk_lambda_obs:.6g}"
        return lines, payload

The Problem

Derive the ideal gas law PV = nRT from kinetic theory, and check it numerically for n = 1 mol of gas at T = 273.15 K in V = 0.022414 m³ (R = 8.314 J/(mol·K)).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Kinetic theory: gas molecules of mass m and mean square speed <c²> strike the walls; the pressure is P = (1/3)×(N/V)×m×<c²>. Step 2: The mean kinetic energy per molecule is (1/2)m<c²> = (3/2)kT, where k is Boltzmann's constant and T the absolute temperature. Step 3: Substituting gives P = NkT/V, and with N = n×N_A and R = N_A×k, this is PV = nRT. Step 4: Numerical check: P = 1 × 8.314 × 273.15 / 0.022414 = 101,325 Pa. Step 5: That is one atmosphere, as expected for one mole at standard temperature in the molar volume. That completes the worked solution; the answer is P = 101,325 Pa.

The Answer

Derived: PV = nRT. Numerical check: one mole at 273.15 K in 0.022414 m³ gives P = 101,325 Pa (one atmosphere). That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. P * V = n * R * T — P is the pressure (Pa); V is the volume (0.022414 m³); n is the amount (1 mol); R is the gas constant (8.314 J/(mol·K)); T is the absolute temperature (273.15 K). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains P = 101,325 Pa, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes point molecules with no intermolecular forces and perfectly elastic collisions; real gases deviate at high pressure and low temperature, where the van der Waals correction applies. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes point molecules with no intermolecular forces and perfectly elastic collisions; real gases deviate at high pressure and low temperature, where the van der Waals correction applies. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the ideal gas law from kinetic theory', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Problem

Derive the ideal gas law PV = nRT from kinetic theory, and check it numerically for n = 1 mol of gas at T = 273.15 K in V = 0.022414 m³ (R = 8.314 J/(mol·K)).

Method

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Kinetic theory: gas molecules of mass m and mean square speed <c²> strike the walls; the pressure is P = (1/3)×(N/V)×m×<c²>. Step 2: The mean kinetic energy per molecule is (1/2)m<c²> = (3/2)kT, where k is Boltzmann's constant and T the absolute temperature. Step 3: Substituting gives P = NkT/V, and with N = n×N_A and R = N_A×k, this is PV = nRT. Step 4: Numerical check: P = 1 × 8.314 × 273.15 / 0.022414 = 101,325 Pa. Step 5: That is one atmosphere, as expected for one mole at standard temperature in the molar volume. That completes the worked solution; the answer is P = 101,325 Pa.

Dataset

Stated numerical values for the check (see key figures); physical constants (g, G, R, h, k, epsilon_0) from the standard references listed in sources.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
n = 1.0
R = 8.314
T = 273.15
V = 0.022414
P = n * R * T / V
_expected_P = 101325.0
_check_P = abs(P - _expected_P) <= 10.0
lines = [
    f"n = {n:.2f} mol",
    f"R = {R:.3f} J/(mol K)",
    f"T = {T:.2f} K",
    f"V = {V:.6f} m^3",
    f"P = {P:.0f} Pa",
    f"Check P against stated value {_expected_P:.0f} Pa: {'PASS' if _check_P else 'FAIL'}",
]
payload = f"P={P:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the ideal gas law from kinetic theory — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        n = 1.0
        R = 8.314
        T = 273.15
        V = 0.022414
        P = n * R * T / V
        _expected_P = 101325.0
        _check_P = abs(P - _expected_P) <= 10.0
        lines = [
            f"n = {n:.2f} mol",
            f"R = {R:.3f} J/(mol K)",
            f"T = {T:.2f} K",
            f"V = {V:.6f} m^3",
            f"P = {P:.0f} Pa",
            f"Check P against stated value {_expected_P:.0f} Pa: {'PASS' if _check_P else 'FAIL'}",
        ]
        payload = f"P={P:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

The Problem

Derive the de Broglie wavelength λ = h/p and check it numerically for an electron (m = 9.109×10⁻³¹ kg) moving at v = 1×10⁶ m/s (h = 6.626×10⁻³⁴ J·s).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: De Broglie proposed that matter has a wavelength given by the same relation as light: λ = h/p, where p is the momentum. Step 2: For a non-relativistic particle the momentum is p = mv. Step 3: Combining gives λ = h/(mv). Step 4: Numerical check: p = 9.109×10⁻³¹ × 1×10⁶ = 9.109×10⁻²⁵ kg·m/s. Step 5: So λ = 6.626×10⁻³⁴ / 9.109×10⁻²⁵ = 7.27×10⁻¹⁰ m. That completes the worked solution; the answer is λ = 7.27×10⁻¹⁰ m.

The Answer

Derived: λ = h/p. Numerical check: an electron at 1×10⁶ m/s has λ = 7.27×10⁻¹⁰ m. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the de Broglie wavelength λ = h/p — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        m = 9.109e-31
        v = 1000000.0
        h = 6.626e-34
        p = m * v
        _expected_p = 9.11e-25
        _check_p = abs(p - _expected_p) <= 1e-26
        lam = h / p
        _expected_lam = 7.27e-10
        _check_lam = abs(lam - _expected_lam) <= 1e-11
        lines = [
            f"m = {m:.3e} kg",
            f"v = {v:.3e} m/s",
            f"h = {h:.3e} J s",
            f"p = {p:.2e} kg m/s",
            f"Check p against stated value {_expected_p:.2e} kg m/s: {'PASS' if _check_p else 'FAIL'}",
            f"lam = {lam:.2e} m",
            f"Check lam against stated value {_expected_lam:.2e} m: {'PASS' if _check_lam else 'FAIL'}",
        ]
        payload = f"p={p:.6g}; lam={lam:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the time-dilation factor from the light clock'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the time-dilation factor from the light clock and check it numerically for a clock moving at v = 0.6c.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A light clock ticks when a light pulse travels up and down between two mirrors a distance L apart; at rest the round trip takes Δt0 = 2L/c. Step 2: For a clock moving at speed v, the light follows a diagonal path: by Pythagoras, the half-trip length is √(L² + (vΔt/2)²). Step 3: The moving round-trip time is Δt = 2×√(L² + (vΔt/2)²)/c; solving for Δt gives Δt = Δt0/√(1 − v²/c²). Step 4: The factor γ = 1/√(1 − v²/c²) is the time-dilation factor. Step 5: Numerical check: at v = 0.6c, γ = 1/√(1 − 0.36) = 1/0.8 = 1.25, so a moving clock runs 1.25 times slower. That completes the worked solution; the answer is γ = 1.25.

The Answer

Derived: Δt = γΔt0 with γ = 1/√(1 − v²/c²). Numerical check: at v = 0.6c, γ = 1.25. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. gamma = 1 / sqrt(1 - v^2 / c^2) — gamma is the Lorentz factor; v is the clock's speed (0.6c); c is the speed of light; Δt is the dilated time and Δt0 the proper time. Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains γ = 1.25, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes the constancy of the speed of light (special relativity) and inertial frames; it applies to the moving clock's proper time and says nothing about mechanical clock faults. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
beta = 0.6
gamma = 1.0 / math.sqrt(1.0 - beta ** 2)
_expected_gamma = 1.25
_check_gamma = abs(gamma - _expected_gamma) <= 0.001
_chk_gamma_sq_check = gamma ** 2 * (1.0 - beta ** 2)
_exp_gamma_sq_check = 1.0
_ok_gamma_sq_check = abs(_chk_gamma_sq_check - _exp_gamma_sq_check) <= 0.001
lines = [
    f"beta = {beta:.2f} v/c",
    f"gamma = {gamma:.4f} ",
    f"Check gamma against stated value {_expected_gamma:.4f} : {'PASS' if _check_gamma else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check gamma_sq_check: {'PASS' if _ok_gamma_sq_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_gamma_sq_check:.4f}, expected {_exp_gamma_sq_check:.4f})",
]
payload = f"gamma={gamma:.6g}; gamma_sq_check={_chk_gamma_sq_check:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the time-dilation factor from the light clock — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        beta = 0.6
        gamma = 1.0 / math.sqrt(1.0 - beta ** 2)
        _expected_gamma = 1.25
        _check_gamma = abs(gamma - _expected_gamma) <= 0.001
        _chk_gamma_sq_check = gamma ** 2 * (1.0 - beta ** 2)
        _exp_gamma_sq_check = 1.0
        _ok_gamma_sq_check = abs(_chk_gamma_sq_check - _exp_gamma_sq_check) <= 0.001
        lines = [
            f"beta = {beta:.2f} v/c",
            f"gamma = {gamma:.4f} ",
            f"Check gamma against stated value {_expected_gamma:.4f} : {'PASS' if _check_gamma else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check gamma_sq_check: {'PASS' if _ok_gamma_sq_check else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_gamma_sq_check:.4f}, expected {_exp_gamma_sq_check:.4f})",
        ]
        payload = f"gamma={gamma:.6g}; gamma_sq_check={_chk_gamma_sq_check:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the range of a projectile R = v²sin2θ/g'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the range R = v²sin2θ/g of a projectile launched over level ground, and check it numerically for v = 20 m/s at θ = 45° (g = 9.81 m/s²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Resolve the launch velocity: horizontal component v×cos(θ), vertical component v×sin(θ). Step 2: The time of flight follows from the vertical motion: the projectile returns to the ground when v×sin(θ)×t − ½gt² = 0, giving t = 2v×sin(θ)/g. Step 3: The range is horizontal speed times time: R = v×cos(θ) × 2v×sin(θ)/g. Step 4: Using 2sin(θ)cos(θ) = sin(2θ) gives R = v²sin(2θ)/g. Step 5: Numerical check: R = 400 × sin(90°)/9.81 = 400/9.81 = 40.77 m; the maximum range occurs at 45°, where sin(2θ) = 1. That completes the worked solution; the answer is R = 40.77 m.

The Answer

Derived: R = v²sin2θ/g. Numerical check: at v = 20 m/s and 45°, R = 40.77 m. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. R = v^2 * sin(2 * theta) / g — R is the range (m); v is the launch speed (20 m/s); theta is the launch angle (45°); g is gravity (9.81 m/s²). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains R = 40.77 m, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes level ground, no air resistance, and constant g; with drag the range is shorter and the best angle is below 45°. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

import hashlib
import math
v = 20.0
theta = 45.0
g = 9.81
R = v ** 2 * math.sin(2.0 * math.radians(theta)) / g
_expected_R = 40.77
_check_R = abs(R - _expected_R) <= 0.01
_chk_t_flight = 2.0 * v * math.sin(math.radians(theta)) / g
_exp_t_flight = 2.88
_ok_t_flight = abs(_chk_t_flight - _exp_t_flight) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"theta = {theta:.2f} deg",
    f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
    f"R = {R:.2f} m",
    f"Check R against stated value {_expected_R:.2f} m: {'PASS' if _check_R else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check t_flight: {'PASS' if _ok_t_flight else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_t_flight:.2f}, expected {_exp_t_flight:.2f})",
]
payload = f"R={R:.6g}; t_flight={_chk_t_flight:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the range of a projectile R = v²sin2θ/g — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        v = 20.0
        theta = 45.0
        g = 9.81
        R = v ** 2 * math.sin(2.0 * math.radians(theta)) / g
        _expected_R = 40.77
        _check_R = abs(R - _expected_R) <= 0.01
        _chk_t_flight = 2.0 * v * math.sin(math.radians(theta)) / g
        _exp_t_flight = 2.88
        _ok_t_flight = abs(_chk_t_flight - _exp_t_flight) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"theta = {theta:.2f} deg",
            f"g = {g:.2f} m/s^2",
            f"R = {R:.2f} m",
            f"Check R against stated value {_expected_R:.2f} m: {'PASS' if _check_R else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check t_flight: {'PASS' if _ok_t_flight else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_t_flight:.2f}, expected {_exp_t_flight:.2f})",
        ]
        payload = f"R={R:.6g}; t_flight={_chk_t_flight:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Derive the speed of a wave on a string v = √(T/μ) JAH-PSPD-000021 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: the answer is recomputed by the demo from the stated inputs and compared with the stated value (PASS), and the relation is confirmed by the cited authoritative sources.

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the speed of a wave on a string v = √(T/μ)'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the speed of a wave on a string v = √(T/μ)', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the speed of a wave on a string v = √(T/μ)'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the speed of a transverse wave on a string, v = √(T/μ), and check it numerically for tension T = 100 N and linear mass density μ = 0.01 kg/m.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Consider a small segment of the string: the transverse restoring force comes from the tension acting at the segment's ends at slightly different angles. Step 2: Applying Newton's second law to the segment gives the wave equation, with the wave speed squared equal to tension over mass per unit length. Step 3: Hence v = √(T/μ). Step 4: Numerical check: v = √(100/0.01) = √(10,000) = 100 m/s. Step 5: Doubling the tension raises the speed by √2; doubling the density lowers it by the same factor. That completes the worked solution; the answer is v = 100 m/s.

The Answer

Derived: v = √(T/μ). Numerical check: with T = 100 N and μ = 0.01 kg/m, v = 100 m/s. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. v = sqrt(T / mu) — v is the wave speed (m/s); T is the string tension (100 N); mu is the linear mass density (0.01 kg/m). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains v = 100 m/s, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes small amplitudes, a uniform string, and negligible stiffness; a real string's stiffness adds dispersion, and the result is for transverse waves only. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes small amplitudes, a uniform string, and negligible stiffness; a real string's stiffness adds dispersion, and the result is for transverse waves only. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
T = 100.0
mu = 0.01
v = math.sqrt(T / mu)
_expected_v = 100.0
_check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
lines = [
    f"T = {T:.2f} N",
    f"mu = {mu:.3f} kg/m",
    f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
    f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
]
payload = f"v={v:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the speed of a wave on a string v = √(T/μ) — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        T = 100.0
        mu = 0.01
        v = math.sqrt(T / mu)
        _expected_v = 100.0
        _check_v = abs(v - _expected_v) <= 0.01
        lines = [
            f"T = {T:.2f} N",
            f"mu = {mu:.3f} kg/m",
            f"v = {v:.2f} m/s",
            f"Check v against stated value {_expected_v:.2f} m/s: {'PASS' if _check_v else 'FAIL'}",
        ]
        payload = f"v={v:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Derive the electric field of a point charge from Coulomb's law JAH-PSPD-000022 · simple

Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the electric field of a point charge from Coulomb's law'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the electric field E = kQ/r² of a point charge from Coulomb's law, and check it numerically for Q = 1 µC at r = 0.1 m (k = 8.988×10⁹ N·m²/C²).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Coulomb's law gives the force between two charges: F = k×Q×q/r². Step 2: The electric field is defined as the force per unit test charge: E = F/q. Step 3: Dividing Coulomb's law by q gives E = kQ/r², directed away from a positive charge. Step 4: Numerical check: E = 8.988×10⁹ × 1×10⁻⁶ / 0.1² = 8.988×10⁹ × 1×10⁻⁶ / 0.01 = 8.99×10⁵ N/C. Step 5: The field falls with the square of the distance, as the inverse-square law requires. That completes the worked solution; the answer is E = 8.99×10⁵ N/C.

The Answer

Derived: E = kQ/r². Numerical check: for Q = 1 µC at 0.1 m, E = 8.99×10⁵ N/C. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. E = k * Q / r^2 — E is the electric field (N/C); k is Coulomb's constant (8.988×10⁹); Q is the source charge (1×10⁻⁶ C); r is the distance (0.1 m). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains E = 8.99×10⁵ N/C, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes a point charge in vacuum (or air) and a stationary test charge; inside matter the field is modified by the dielectric, and moving charges add magnetic effects. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the electric field of a point charge from Coulomb's law — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        k = 8988000000.0
        Q = 1e-06
        r = 0.1
        E = k * Q / r ** 2
        _expected_E = 898800.0
        _check_E = abs(E - _expected_E) <= 100.0
        _chk_F_on_1uC = E * 1e-06
        _exp_F_on_1uC = 0.8988
        _ok_F_on_1uC = abs(_chk_F_on_1uC - _exp_F_on_1uC) <= 0.001
        lines = [
            f"k = {k:.3e} SI",
            f"Q = {Q:.2e} C",
            f"r = {r:.2f} m",
            f"E = {E:.0f} N/C",
            f"Check E against stated value {_expected_E:.0f} N/C: {'PASS' if _check_E else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check F_on_1uC: {'PASS' if _ok_F_on_1uC else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_F_on_1uC:.4f}, expected {_exp_F_on_1uC:.4f})",
        ]
        payload = f"E={E:.6g}; F_on_1uC={_chk_F_on_1uC:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

History

Derivations of this form are the backbone of classical and modern physics as taught in the standard university texts, above all the OpenStax University Physics sequence and the treatments collected in HyperPhysics. Each one begins from a definition or a conservation law and ends at a formula that working physicists and engineers use daily. This instance, 'Derive the capacitance of a parallel-plate capacitor', is derived here from first principles with fixed numerical values for the check, so that every step can be followed and the final formula tested against a concrete case.

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the capacitance of a parallel-plate capacitor'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the capacitance C = ε₀A/d of a parallel-plate capacitor, and check it numerically for A = 0.01 m² and d = 0.001 m (ε₀ = 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m).

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: A charge +Q on one plate and −Q on the other produces a uniform field between the plates: E = σ/ε₀, where σ = Q/A is the surface charge density. Step 2: The potential difference is V = E×d = Q×d/(ε₀×A). Step 3: Capacitance is defined as C = Q/V, so C = ε₀×A/d. Step 4: Numerical check: C = 8.854×10⁻¹² × 0.01 / 0.001 = 8.85×10⁻¹¹ F. Step 5: That is 88.5 pF, a typical value for a small air-gap capacitor. That completes the worked solution; the answer is C = 8.85×10⁻¹¹ F.

The Answer

Derived: C = ε₀A/d. Numerical check: with A = 0.01 m² and d = 0.001 m, C = 8.85×10⁻¹¹ F (88.5 pF). That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

When It Applies

This record applies whenever the stated problem is the problem in front of you: the same question, the same configuration, the same stated inputs. The solution is checked in three ways. First, the worked solution shows every step, so a reader can follow the arithmetic or the table row by row and find any slip. Second, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs in code and prints the result together with a SHA-256 digest of the computed payload, run twice, with a Deterministic: MATCH line confirming the two runs agree; the demo also compares the computed answer with the stated value and prints PASS when they agree. Third, the program repeats the same computation as a reusable routine, so the answer can be regenerated on demand. The authoritative sources listed below confirm the relation and the constants used.

Limits

The answer holds only inside its stated conditions. The derivation holds only under its stated assumptions: the idealisations named in the solution (constant acceleration, no friction, point masses, ideal gases, and similar) limit where the result applies, and outside those conditions the formula must be corrected or replaced. Beyond those conditions the answer changes or stops applying: different inputs give different answers, and the record's numbers must not be carried into a different configuration without redoing the work. Nothing here is a design approval, a safety certification, or a claim about a physical system that was never measured. The record proves what it states about the stated problem and no more, and any use beyond that problem should repeat the computation with the new data.

Math Proof

The mathematical justification is the chain of algebra from the starting principle to the final formula. C = epsilon_0 * A / d — C is the capacitance (F); epsilon_0 is the permittivity of free space (8.854×10⁻¹² F/m); A is the plate area (0.01 m²); d is the plate separation (0.001 m). Each step is a substitution, a rearrangement, or an integration of a stated relation, and the worked solution shows every one. The numerical check then evaluates the final formula for the stated values and obtains C = 8.85×10⁻¹¹ F, which the demo recomputes in code and compares with the stated value. The derivation assumes a uniform field and neglects fringing at the plate edges; it holds when the plate separation is small compared with the plate size, and a dielectric changes ε₀ to ε. The derivation and the check agree, which is the verification this record offers.

Science Proof

The derivation is scientifically sound because each step follows from a stated principle or a definition, and no step smuggles in the conclusion. The derivation assumes a uniform field and neglects fringing at the plate edges; it holds when the plate separation is small compared with the plate size, and a dielectric changes ε₀ to ε. The final formula is then tested numerically: the demo evaluates it for the stated values and checks it against an independent computation of the same quantity, so an algebraic slip would show up as a failed check. The record states where the derivation's assumptions hold — the idealisations, the ranges, and the regimes where the formula stops applying — so the result is used only inside its domain.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
eps0 = 8.854e-12
A = 0.01
d = 0.001
C = eps0 * A / d
_expected_C = 8.85e-11
_check_C = abs(C - _expected_C) <= 1e-12
_chk_C_pF = C * 1e12
_exp_C_pF = 88.5
_ok_C_pF = abs(_chk_C_pF - _exp_C_pF) <= 0.1
lines = [
    f"eps0 = {eps0:.3e} F/m",
    f"A = {A:.3f} m^2",
    f"d = {d:.4f} m",
    f"C = {C:.2e} F",
    f"Check C against stated value {_expected_C:.2e} F: {'PASS' if _check_C else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check C_pF: {'PASS' if _ok_C_pF else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_C_pF:.1f}, expected {_exp_C_pF:.1f})",
]
payload = f"C={C:.6g}; C_pF={_chk_C_pF:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the capacitance of a parallel-plate capacitor — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        eps0 = 8.854e-12
        A = 0.01
        d = 0.001
        C = eps0 * A / d
        _expected_C = 8.85e-11
        _check_C = abs(C - _expected_C) <= 1e-12
        _chk_C_pF = C * 1e12
        _exp_C_pF = 88.5
        _ok_C_pF = abs(_chk_C_pF - _exp_C_pF) <= 0.1
        lines = [
            f"eps0 = {eps0:.3e} F/m",
            f"A = {A:.3f} m^2",
            f"d = {d:.4f} m",
            f"C = {C:.2e} F",
            f"Check C against stated value {_expected_C:.2e} F: {'PASS' if _check_C else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check C_pF: {'PASS' if _ok_C_pF else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_C_pF:.1f}, expected {_exp_C_pF:.1f})",
        ]
        payload = f"C={C:.6g}; C_pF={_chk_C_pF:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Description

This record presolves the physics derivation 'Derive the half-life decay law N = N₀e^(−λt)'. It starts from the stated first principles, carries the derivation through every algebraic step with nothing skipped, states the final formula, and then checks that formula against a worked numerical example. A derivation is the purest presolved shape: the answer is not a recalled fact but a result proved from principles, and the record keeps the proof and the check side by side so that an AI retrieving it gets both the reasoning and the evidence. The runnable demo re-performs the derivation's numerical check in code, so the final formula is verified by execution as well as by algebra.

The Problem

Derive the decay law N = N₀e^(−λt) from the constant decay probability per nucleus, and check it numerically for N₀ = 1,000 nuclei with a half-life of 10 years after t = 30 years.

The Worked Solution

The solution proceeds in the following steps. Step 1: Each nucleus has a constant probability λ per unit time of decaying, so the number decaying per unit time is λN. Step 2: That gives the differential equation dN/dt = −λN. Step 3: Separating variables and integrating gives ln(N/N₀) = −λt, hence N = N₀e^(−λt). Step 4: The half-life is T = ln(2)/λ, so λ = ln(2)/10 = 0.0693 per year. Step 5: Numerical check: N = 1,000 × e^(−0.0693×30) = 1,000 × e^(−2.079) = 1,000 × 0.125 = 125 nuclei, exactly one eighth after three half-lives. That completes the worked solution; the answer is N = 125.

The Answer

Derived: N = N₀e^(−λt). Numerical check: 1,000 nuclei with a 10-year half-life fall to 125 after 30 years. That value is the numerical check of the derived formula for the stated inputs; the formula itself is the derivation's answer, and the number shows the formula working on a concrete case.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
N0 = 1000.0
T_half = 10.0
t = 30.0
lam = math.log(2.0) / T_half
_expected_lam = 0.0693
_check_lam = abs(lam - _expected_lam) <= 0.0001
N = N0 * math.exp(-lam * t)
_expected_N = 125.0
_check_N = abs(N - _expected_N) <= 0.1
_chk_N_eighths = N0 / 8.0
_exp_N_eighths = 125.0
_ok_N_eighths = abs(_chk_N_eighths - _exp_N_eighths) <= 0.1
lines = [
    f"N0 = {N0:.0f} ",
    f"T_half = {T_half:.2f} y",
    f"t = {t:.2f} y",
    f"lam = {lam:.4f} per y",
    f"Check lam against stated value {_expected_lam:.4f} per y: {'PASS' if _check_lam else 'FAIL'}",
    f"N = {N:.1f} ",
    f"Check N against stated value {_expected_N:.1f} : {'PASS' if _check_N else 'FAIL'}",
    f"Check N_eighths: {'PASS' if _ok_N_eighths else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_N_eighths:.1f}, expected {_exp_N_eighths:.1f})",
]
payload = f"lam={lam:.6g}; N={N:.6g}; N_eighths={_chk_N_eighths:.6g}"
for _line in lines:
    print(_line)
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")

Full working program

import hashlib


class PresolvedSolution:
    """Derive the half-life decay law N = N₀e^(−λt) — presolved solution. run() recomputes the answer from the stated inputs."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        N0 = 1000.0
        T_half = 10.0
        t = 30.0
        lam = math.log(2.0) / T_half
        _expected_lam = 0.0693
        _check_lam = abs(lam - _expected_lam) <= 0.0001
        N = N0 * math.exp(-lam * t)
        _expected_N = 125.0
        _check_N = abs(N - _expected_N) <= 0.1
        _chk_N_eighths = N0 / 8.0
        _exp_N_eighths = 125.0
        _ok_N_eighths = abs(_chk_N_eighths - _exp_N_eighths) <= 0.1
        lines = [
            f"N0 = {N0:.0f} ",
            f"T_half = {T_half:.2f} y",
            f"t = {t:.2f} y",
            f"lam = {lam:.4f} per y",
            f"Check lam against stated value {_expected_lam:.4f} per y: {'PASS' if _check_lam else 'FAIL'}",
            f"N = {N:.1f} ",
            f"Check N against stated value {_expected_N:.1f} : {'PASS' if _check_N else 'FAIL'}",
            f"Check N_eighths: {'PASS' if _ok_N_eighths else 'FAIL'} (computed {_chk_N_eighths:.1f}, expected {_exp_N_eighths:.1f})",
        ]
        payload = f"lam={lam:.6g}; N={N:.6g}; N_eighths={_chk_N_eighths:.6g}"
        return lines, payload


if __name__ == "__main__":
    _solution = PresolvedSolution()
    _lines, _payload = _solution.run()
    for _line in _lines:
        print(_line)
    _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {_h1}")
    print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {_h2}")
    print("Deterministic: MATCH" if _h1 == _h2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
